ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U1	Dear Riigikogu, good afternoon. Question Time. Mr. Prime Minister, Question Time starts at 1:00. Incoming check, please! 36 members of the Riigikogu are present in Question Time. Evgeni Ossinovski, Minister of Finance Sven Sester and Minister of the Interior, Andres Anvelt, will be in briefing today. Yesterday, ten members of the Riigikogu expressed their wish to ask a question, so I will give you the opportunity to ask a specific question during the debate on each topic.
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U2	The first question is for Mr Ossinovski, Minister of Health and Labour. Please, Urve Tiidus!
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U3	Thank you, Mr President. Minister, in his duties as Prime Minister. Mark Twain in the 19th century said that if you don't read the papers, you don't know anything, but if you do, you know everything wrong. This is, of course, a distant history, but my question is motivated by an article just published in the Saaremaa newspaper, from which one member of your government can read a somewhat controversial view of administrative reform. As we know, the local authorities of Saaremaa have signed the merger decision, but the minister was said to have regretted that he was late in a later interview, and he felt that it would have been better to divide Saaremaa into three. Since one local authority did not join this agreement, I will ask you two questions. Does the government think that this exceptional situation, if one stays separate, is a good one? How do you comment on your colleague's view that the administrative reform in Saaremaa could have taken place in a different way?
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U4	Thank you, Honourable Asker. The government has clearly and unambiguously said that the administrative reform will go on and that the criteria laid down in the law will not be changed. This concerns both the population criterion and the timetable, of course, with a court reservation in mind. As we know, the Supreme Court will make its decision on administrative reform on 20 December, and of course it may affect the activities of the government, the Riigikogu and also local governments. I am undoubtedly pleased that in Saaremaa a consensus and willingness has been found to become a county-sized municipality. I think it's good and reasonable. In general, this is, I think, one of the best examples of the voluntary administrative reform process. In any case, I congratulate these municipalities for the work they have done. I must honestly admit that I have also read in the newspaper that in some private conversations a member of the Government of the Republic has thought something else, but I do not consider it possible to comment more precisely, because I really cannot control the credibility of this statement or the broader background. On behalf of the Government of the Republic, I can say that the administrative reform is moving forward within previously agreed frameworks.
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U5	Urve Tiidus, a more specific question, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U6	Thank you for your answer. Minister, I would like to ask for clarification. You have not commented on this exception at the moment. If you could do that, it would be very nice. But how do you generally predict the progress of the administrative reform process, both with regard to existing laws and regulations and with regard to deadlines?
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U7	Thank you. If you ask for further details, ask for this exception separately. I'm sorry, but I didn't exactly notice what exception we were talking about. But as for the wider implementation of the administrative reform, as foreseen in the timetable, the phase of voluntary mergers will be over by the end of this year. Then, in the second stage, the Government of the Republic joins those municipalities that do not meet the criteria. Here's the procedure. The Government of the Republic shall submit the application to the local government by 15 February at the latest and the Government shall be able to express its opinion on it until May. Then, when all these opinions are received, the government will make its decisions. Forced mergers will take place at the same time as voluntary mergers, which will be completed by the time of the local elections, by October 2017. It can be said that the voluntary merger phase has generally gone well. The Government of the Republic has already confirmed two accession decisions, which concern the realms of Saue and Tõrva. Accession negotiations are held in most of Estonia's municipalities. Some individuals do not participate in the accession negotiations, most likely waiting for the Supreme Court decision of 20 December, as some of them have contested the constitutionality of the Administrative Reform Act. The second part is either completing the accession negotiations or has already completed them, but they still do not meet the criteria after that. They are dealt with by the Government of the Republic according to the law.

ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U9	Thank you, Mr. Chairman. Ladies and gentlemen, the acting Prime Minister. ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U10	Thank you. In the cases you mentioned, as well as in general terms, local rulers have agreed on names during the accession negotiations on the basis of the mandate given to them. I have no competence to assess whether these names are adequate or not. Historically developed names certainly have a traditional symbolic value, but I think that the name does not generally violate the local government. It is certainly not worth taking names that might mislead you. In previous practice, such a concern was once when a municipality in Saaremaa wanted to have the name of Saaremaa municipality. I think it would have been a little misleading for the rest of the municipalities. But whether it can be agreed that the name of an existing municipality will be used more widely or that a new name will be introduced, and whether it is acceptable to everyone is, in my opinion, a matter of local autonomy. I would not say that this is the most burning or important issue of administrative reform.
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U11	I will conclude on this issue.
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U12	The next question is for Finance Minister Sven Sester. Arthur Talvik, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U13	Thank you. Good minister! In 2002, the hydroelectric power plant in Linnamäe was restored. This was done with the support of 345 people and a few dozen companies. This power plant was built in 1924 and the State put EUR 22 million in its restoration. It's produced a nice amount of energy, but now it's planning to break down an active hydropower plant. As a person in the role of the general meeting of Eesti Energia, have you made a decision to shut it down, and if you have done so, what will you say to these 345 people? Will you pay them back the money?
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U14	Thank you, dear questioner. A true story was completed in 2002, which has become Estonia's largest hydropower plant today. To this end, Eesti Energia carried out the first cooperation project in Eastern Europe, offering renewable energy products to final consumers under the name green energy. As you rightly said, 345 individuals and 20 companies joined the campaign. The total revenue of the campaign was 77300 euros, of which 12,000 were donated to the Estonian Nature Foundation, who was a co-author of the project. The remaining EUR 65300 accounted for 3.4% of the total investment in the hydroelectric power plant in Linnamäe. Eesti Energia was also obliged to restore the hydropower plant. It's worked beautifully for 15 years. Looking back in time, it is clear that some changes have taken place since 2002. In 2004, the Minister of the Environment issued a regulation amending the Jägala River from the waterfall to the waterfall into a storage area in the Gulf of Finland. In 2005, the Government of the Republic adopted a decision to label it as a Natura site. Today we are in a position where we need to ensure access to fish, both upstream and downstream. The Minister for the Environment has also made a separate submission that we should carry out tasks related to the Natura site.
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U15	Specific question, Artur Talvik, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U16	Good minister! I had a very specific question. As the general meeting of Eesti Energia, have you taken the decision to shut down this efficient hydroelectric power plant, which provides energy to some 3,000 households, a historic object? As a reminder, after a mile, Jägala Falls will come up against it anyway, and this hydropower plant has been operating there for almost a hundred years. Once again, as your general meeting, have you decided to shut down the hydroelectric power plant of Eesti Energia, which is, as you said, the largest hydroelectric power plant in Estonia? What are the resulting decisions?
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U17	Thank you, dear questioner. Several parties have dealt with this issue: Environmental Board, Ministry of Environment, Minister of Finance, Ministry of Finance and Eesti Energia. True story, estimates have been made for today, what the volume of investments in Eesti Energia has been during this period and what profitability has been. All previous decisions that force the Government of the Republic and the Estonian state as a whole to protect Natura sites have been taken into account. It has now been agreed that the existing hydroelectric power plant will, let's say, proceed with the liquidation. There is specific information that a specific installation does not need to be dismantled in order to meet conservation objectives. Right now, I understand, the Minister for the Environment and the Minister for Culture are working to make it possible to register as a cultural monument. According to my data, all parties wish to achieve two opposite objectives to the maximum: that fish can move downstream and upstream and that the historic facility can be maintained. Working for these 'controversial' wishes, I have understood, the Minister for the Environment is also clearly in the process; this task is on his desk. So today, action is being taken to fulfil the commitments made by the Estonian state in Natura sites. If this makes it necessary to shut down the hydroelectric power plant, but the plant can still be maintained, work will be done to this end.
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U18	The last point to be specified. Andres Herkel, please!

ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U22	The next question is for Home Secretary Andres Anvelt. Henn Põllamaas, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U23	Thank you, Mr. Chairman. Good minister! The Soots are known for trying to force society to impose things that the party's programme documents are silent about and which have not been spoken about in the elections. I am thinking, for example, of what is called hate speech. The Constitution guarantees freedom of conscience, thought, and also freedom of speech, which would be brutally violated by criminalising hate speech. All the more so since incitement to hatred is already prohibited and there is no specific need to ban hate speech. Such efforts are not driven by the need of society, but rather by the desire to muzzle opponents. Once we've been in a situation like this. Then there was a monopoly on truth, and all dissidents were punished, let alone dissidents. You have taken the lead in this matter, and you are now the Minister of the Interior. Do the socialists intend to move on to the subject again?
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U24	Thank you, good questioner, for the question. First of all, I would point out that the amendment, organisation and revision of the Penal Code, as well as its alignment with the European Union Framework Decisions, for example, are within the competence of the Ministry of Justice; the Ministry of the Interior does not come up with these initiatives. But now I promise myself a little rhetoric, because I have taken part in many social media debates myself, and I want to talk about what hate speech is all about. As Minister of Justice, I was confronted with the development of such a possible provision. This provision has not reached any point now, but its development may be called an inclusion project, as it has been discussed for four years at a number of round tables. But I'll just tell you a few sentences, think about them. When a public call is made for violence against a particular group of people or a member of a group, it is an hate speech. I think that no group in society deserves the public calling on them either to lynch, beat or kill them, then these people should not be protected by the state. This hate speech provision, which has been developed in the Ministry of Justice during various ministerial times, does not lead to the restriction of the freedom of expression that you fear, it does not simply restrict your opinion.
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U25	Henn Põllaas, a more specific question, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U26	I'm glad to hear the Home Office isn't coming up with this initiative. ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U27	Thank you. I'll explain it again. ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U28	Jaak Madison, a more specific question, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U29	Thank you, Mr President of the Riigikogu. Good minister! I understand your point, but I have two questions. ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U30	Time! Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U31	Thank you. I certainly agree that the way of overregulation must not go under any circumstances. I think that is why four years have been discussing whether this act should be criminalised or misdemeanored. The Ministry of Justice must continue to deal with this issue. However, I do think that in the existing Penal Code it would be necessary to supplement the catalogue of aggravating circumstances so that an aggravating circumstance would also be regarded as committing a criminal offence against someone because of some distinctive sign of that person or group of people. For example, several years ago there was a case when the youngsters killed, I don't want to say for fun, but there was no other motivation found for social outcasts. So they beat them and killed them. The actions of these young men could not be regarded as a hate crime. It is also the case when, for example, someone who is shorter in size or has a different skin color or whatever gets beaten on the street. If he is being assaulted by an old-fashioned hooliganic motive, this crime cannot be treated as a more serious crime and is being treated more leniently than if a person had been taken from his pocket as a result of his beating. This is something that needs more dialogue. Today's Minister of Justice has also said from this remote that this must be considered. But again, I stress that whether or not this wider discussion must continue is not included in the coalition agreement today. These decisions must be made by the Ministry of Justice.

ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U34	Thank you, good manager. Good Sven! Let's warm up the bill 302 for today's debate. ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U35	Thank you, dear questioner, good Remo! True, one of the top priorities of this government is economic growth. Economic growth is, I think, the foundation of all things. If there is good economic growth, we will be able to channel money into different areas, from roads to subsidies, defence capabilities and wages. When we look at what measures the government will take in the near future, I mean that there will be two years and a few months, we will see that very much attention will be paid to labour taxes. The same objective, to pay attention to labour taxes, has been before, but now we are doing it even more forcefully. This is compensated by possible taxes related to consumption and vice taxation or the environment. If you want to know more specifically what measures the government will take in the near future, I say that in the near future the government will take steps to increase the tax-free minimum. This is not done by EUR 10, EUR 15 or EUR 20, as has been the case before, but by a strong step, increasing the tax-free minimum to EUR 500 per month for people on lower and medium wages. This creates a situation where people who are paid EUR 500-1200 per month will in the future receive EUR 62 per month more, or just over EUR 700 per year. This is a real relief and will also contribute to employment.
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U36	Remo Holsmer, a more specific question, please!

ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U38	Thank you, dear questioner. I would like you to look at the whole picture and look at both the benefits and the costs. Surely you cannot say that the reduction in labour taxes is not a priority for this government. Rather, ambition is much higher than in the past. If you look at what the government is about to do in the near future, take a look at the cessation of the increase in diesel excise duty in 2018. This is an important input for our Estonian companies. Or, for example, look at the abolition of the increase in VAT on hotels, which, just as the reduction in social tax has not yet entered into force, should have entered into force on 1 January 2017. So the increase to 14% is canceled, the tax stays at 9%, contributing to our export capacity.

ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U44	Thank you, President-in-Office. Dear Minister, This is about Muraste Police and Border Guard School. For some time, the closing of the School of Police and Border Guard College Paikus was on the agenda of the Estonian Academy of Defence. It has now been decided that Paikuse School will not be closed, but will be developed into a police study centre. An investment of EUR 2 to 3 million has been planned. Tallinna Kase street study centre will also remain. The teaching is thus divided between Tallinn and Paikus. The Muraste study centre providing border education is closed, although it is a learning complex at international level. Where do investments come from? What is the purpose of such action? What was the need for Muraste to close school?
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U45	Thank you, good questioner! The decision to close Muraste School was taken already in August 2015. But as a new minister, I took up this issue to explain the reasons behind it. These hindsights are, in my opinion, entirely adequate. Education at the Academy of Internal Defence, as we know, is already expensive because learners need a completely different learning base than is needed in many other universities of applied sciences and sciences. They also need polygons, if I may call them that. One of the reasons for the closure of the school was also the 30% savings in management costs, but this certainly cannot be at the expense of the quality of education. These local investments, which you have spoken about, provide an opportunity to extend important training activities to both police and border guards there. For example, the tactical shooting range will be significantly increased and made safe, the alarm training polygon will certainly be increased and extended and more study rooms will be built. I would not particularly like to point out the very large differences between basic training for police and border guards, because in fact they both do law enforcement, some do them internally, others rather at the border, but basic knowledge is very similar. So the closure of Muraste School provides an opportunity to invest more in other existing schools so that students have better training.

ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U53	Next is the question of Jaak Madison to the Minister for Health and Labour, Jevgeni Ossinovski, in charge of the Prime Minister's duties. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U54	Thank you, Mr President of the Riigikogu. Good minister! A few weeks ago, you expressed your opinion in the public media that your policy is aimed at banning all forms of alcohol advertising, as well as banning "special offers" or tastes of happy hours and alcohol-containing drinks. A brief and specific question: why do you want to turn Estonia into a society of prohibitions and orders, where everything is regulated and people have no chance of living the way they live in a free society? Instead, they must rather live in a left-wing regime, where the state has banned everything and penalises it strictly if the prohibitions are violated.
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U55	Thank you, Honourable Asker. It's always a pleasure to hear your questions. It is heartwarming to see how the Conservative People's Party can move from such extreme conservatism to extremeliberality and backwards. But that is, of course, your right and your freedom, it is my duty to answer the question you have asked. When we are talking about alcohol policy, it must be said that this has indeed been a priority for the Ministry of Social Affairs. We have drawn up proposals to tighten up alcohol policy due to international recommendations and scientific results, as well as the Green Paper on alcohol policy agreed in Estonia. The aim is one: to reduce the total consumption of alcohol in Estonia. We consider minors, as well as over-consumers, as distinctly important target groups. Now we're gonna get to the bottom of this. International studies and practices in other countries say that communication and education in the field of alcohol policy are very good, and this is also worth doing, but without further regulatory measures, there will be no results in alcohol policy without regulatory restrictions. The most effective measures to reduce alcohol consumption are increases in the price of alcohol and restrictions on advertising and restrictions on access to alcohol. All three of them we also apply to reduce the overall consumption of alcohol in Estonian society. In the last 12 seconds, there's still a record of freedom. Freedom is important. With these proposals, we will not restrict the free choice of people. It should be borne in mind, however, that according to conservative estimates, there are about 60,000 alcohol addicts in Estonia and it is not quite right to talk about their freedom to drink alcohol.

ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U84	Honourable Chairman of the Riigikogu! Ladies and gentlemen, At the third reading of the draft law, the Conservative People's Party Group will vote against it. I think it is absolutely necessary to state what are the main reasons for this. We cannot accept the relaxation of the current regulation, which seeks to attract more workers, whether it is for the creation of start-ups. As we know, the concept of start-ups is very broad. There are very many examples of how people have been able to obtain a residence or work permit in the shelter of setting up a start-up company, but their real purpose has not been to contribute to the Estonian economy or to increase our prosperity. In the explanatory memorandum to the draft law, I will mention two points that we cannot agree with under any circumstances. The first point: 'The aim of the draft amendments is to facilitate and facilitate the temporary stay and residence of foreigners who are prepared and able to contribute to the development of Estonia. The wider objective of the proposal is to help achieve national targets in a constantly changing environment, taking into account global and regional processes. ' Because of the low rate of growth and demographic crisis, it is true that as time goes on, the more labour shortages are getting worse, but in Estonia, where 1.3 million people live, barely 900,000 of them Estonians, we cannot begin to tackle labour market problems with foreign labour. Another point we cannot agree with: 'Like other EU Member States, Estonia faces economic and demographic challenges and competes with other economies to attract people with the skills and knowledge needed for the labour market and the business environment. In the last five years, the workforce in Estonia has decreased by 7,000 people, and according to Eurostat forecasts, more than 100,000 people of working age will live less in Estonia after 20 years. ' That is another point where we cannot accept the proposed solution. The solution cannot be to relax the existing regulation, but the solution must be one that motivates the people who have already left it to come back, namely around 100,000 and helps to resolve the demographic crisis, or population crisis. These must be the priorities that we must pursue so that we are no longer in this situation in 20 years' time. Thus, if only 55% of the inhabitants of the Estonian capital are Estonians and if there are fewer than one million Estonians in the whole country, the solution cannot be to copy the model from Western Europe, where the last 40 years have been attempted to solve demographic and labour problems by means of external migration. Conservatives will vote against it. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U85	Liisa Oviir, Speech by the Group of the Social Democratic Party. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U86	The 251 bill makes changes that the Estonian business environment has long been looking forward to. The draft facilitates the introduction of foreigners who contribute to Estonian society and development and can provide a very significant added value to our economy. Although there is much to be commended in this draft, I would like to focus on three changes with a very clear output for our economy. All changes to the bill are linked to supporting productivity growth and innovation, which helps to promote Estonia's competitiveness. Both global and regional processes have been taken into account, in particular internationalisation, as well as the decline in the working population. According to Eurostat forecasts, the working-age population of Estonia will fall by 100000 in the next 20 years. That must be taken into account. It must also be taken into account that Estonia is in competition with other countries in the region, all of whom are trying to attract the best hands. In order to ensure the competitiveness and sustainability of the Estonian economy, it is therefore important to encourage the immigration of talent and entrepreneurs required for the development of the country. The draft therefore creates exceptions for those categories that give a rapid boost to Estonia's competitiveness. These are ICT specialists. ICT is certainly an area of high added value, but ICTs have increasingly been introduced in other sectors of the economy, such as industry, and this creates the need for a suitably qualified workforce. In addition, in recent years Estonia has dealt with attracting ICT companies and their development centres to Estonia, but unfortunately we are no longer able to provide them with enough and suitable quality labour. This change will lead to a significant improvement. In addition to our knowledge of ICT, we are also pioneers in start-ups. For a start-up, contrary to what the previous speaker said, the explanatory memorandum to the draft defines a start-up as an economic entity with a high global growth potential, which aims to develop and start up an innovative and reproducible business model and which contributes significantly to the development of the Estonian economy and the business environment. Estonia has been a good breeding ground for start-ups. Namely, Estonia has the largest number of start-ups per capita throughout the European Union, and on a global scale we are the second largest after Israel. However, international competition has also become increasingly intense here. Start-ups create jobs with high added value, they bring foreign capital to Estonia and they also contribute to the state budget through tax receipts. Apart from boosting growth and creating jobs, creating a favourable environment for start-ups will contribute to the development of our entire technology sector. Lastly, in order to increase the production of foreign investment in Estonia, the draft creates opportunities for granting a permit in a simplified way to large investors. In our competitiveness plan 'Estonia 2020', one of the priorities of the Estonian state is that a better environment must be created to bring foreign investment into Estonia. Under this scheme, in order to attract foreign investment here, it is necessary to offer investors attractive benefits that are competitive in international comparison. The granting of a simplified residence permit also supports the objectives that we set, creating a support measure for a large investor to attract investments into Estonia. This decision has already been signed and the measure will open at EAS on 9 January in a new year starting. The active involvement of large investors in the Estonian economy contributes to increasing the productivity and export potential of companies, increasing the added value of products, opening up new market opportunities and creating jobs that create high added value. More time, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U87	Three minutes extra time.
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U88	The Estonian business environment will also become more attractive to foreigners, which in turn will diversify and strengthen Estonia's competitive position. Thus, the general and main objective of this draft is to improve our business environment by bringing to Estonia precisely the working hands that our economy feels most in need of. To sum it up. This is a very necessary draft for our economic recovery, but, as has been pointed out at the recent Business Day Industrial Conference, these changes are extremely necessary, but they do not solve the shortage of hands in industry. ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U95	Ladies and gentlemen, This draft concerns the introduction of seasonal workers into Estonia and their stay here, i.e. their opportunities to live and work in Estonia will be extended. Student placements will be filled in instantly as soon as registration is open. We have tens of thousands of young people who don't have a job in the summer. This draft has, in my opinion, found a very reasonable compromise: the compulsory rate of pay has been reduced to the average wage in Estonia. This is the optimal solution, I think, for quite a few sectors. Alternatively, on the table, if you remember, there was an opportunity to introduce an average sectoral salary, which some Riigikogu committees considered to be right in their discussions. I think that would certainly have been a worse option. In sectors where we would like to get more people and give them a residence permit to work, the average wage is much higher than the Estonian average. We would therefore not have been able to involve skilled and highly paid workers there. On the other hand, this option might have opened the door that the IRL Group certainly does not want to open, so in those sectors where the average wage is much lower than the Estonian average, the conditions would have been lower than the bar and would have created another unequal situation and the unequal treatment of Estonian resident workers. This optimal solution, which is contained in point 52 of the draft, is, in my opinion, a quite good compromise. Disputes were heated, but in general, this bill with its principles certainly protects the general concepts and principles of conservative migration policy and, on the other hand, opens up opportunities for Estonian employers to recruit more highly paid workers and skilled workers. The IRL Group supports the adoption of the draft as a law. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U112	We have before us a bill setting out the dental benefit. Undoubtedly, the introduction of dental benefits is a noble step, as the dental situation of Estonians is more than bad. Sadly, today, it often depends on whether a person can take care of his teeth as he or she would like to, depending on his or her financial situation. We certainly welcome the solution proposed in this draft for elderly people and those with increased medical needs. However, criticism must be given about the solution offered to people of working age. Why? The Government of the Republic offers that the budget of the Health Insurance Fund will support a person's dental care with a maximum annual amount of 30 euros if the bill is at least 60 euros. Unfortunately, there is a very high risk that if draft 301 is passed as a law in the form that it is now before us, people will not be able to use dental care benefits, but we will make an unprotected promise to people, not a hundred percent, but very much unprotected. I'm trying to explain this. Until the first reading, we had the feeling that this was an excellent initiative. After making the amendments, we also received the views of the Estonian Association of Dentists, which were negative for this draft. In particular, dental benefits are planned in kind. This means that the person receives treatment and the institution issues a bill to the health insurance fund, i.e. the person himself does not pay 30 euros. What happens, though, is that in order to compensate for the cost of the treatment, the health insurance fund must conclude contracts with dentists. Unfortunately, however, the health insurance fund has used a power position and prices are not in line with reality, and the health insurance fund makes price dumping. The Dentists' Association has made it very clear that no contractual partners will be found under such conditions, with 5% of dental care providers entering into a contractual relationship here with the Health Insurance Fund. This means that a person does not really have the opportunity to receive support. He or she must choose a dental care provider who is in a contractual relationship with the health insurance fund; he or she can no longer choose which dentist he or she attends. This is not a normal solution in today's medicine, because there is free movement of patients within the European Union. So what's the solution? The fact that the compensation is financial. The person then chooses a dentist, pays for the treatment and applies to the health insurance fund for compensation. Very simple. The Social Affairs Committee repeatedly proposed that the Health Insurance Fund and the Estonian Dental Association sit down, find common ground and bring positions closer together. Unfortunately, this did not happen; all parties remained in their positions. Unfortunately, the Estonian Free Party Group cannot support the draft. It would have been very easy to make dental benefits financially, as is currently the prosthetic benefit for older people. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U113	Helmen Kütt on behalf of the Social Democratic Party Group, please! Three minutes extra time.
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U114	President-in-Office of the Council. Ladies and gentlemen, What's the situation in dental care, nobody really knows. It is not known how poor Estonian people's teeth are in fact, because there is no competent research on this. According to a survey by Emori last year, only 37% of Estonian population aged 15 to 74 attended dental visits in 2015. Men, 20 to 29 years old, people with lower education and people living in rural areas are the least attending dentists. Most are hampered by economic reasons. They also point out that since there are no complaints, there is no reason to go there more often. Women aged 20 to 39 usually avoid regular dental visits for economic reasons. However, no one disputes that there are strong links between teeth and the whole body's health. The broken teeth damage the liver, kidneys, and heart, according to the doctors. The bad condition of your teeth is often also a sign of a serious illness. At present, dental care in Estonia is free only for persons under the age of 19 years, and for adults, the Health Insurance Fund pays in kind for dental care without applying the patient's own contribution. Pensioners receiving financial compensation and inability to work 19.18 euros a year, and mothers of pregnant and children under one year of age receive compensation of 28.77 euros. Neither amount actually covers the cost of the treatment. Proteese is reimbursed by the Health Insurance Fund for EUR 255,65 for three years. All these benefits must be applied for on the basis of an application, first they must be paid for the treatment themselves, and later the sickness fund transfers the above few amounts to the people's accounts. What Will Change From 1 July 2017? An important difference is that in the future, people will no longer have to apply for compensation themselves, but the whole settlement will take place between the dentists and the health insurance fund. There is no change in the basic allowance for insured persons under 19 years of age for dental care and uninsured adults, i.e. the existing benefits paid for children's dental care and in the case of emergency care do not go away. Moreover, in these cases, the own contribution is not applicable. However, the state will provide more support to persons who have been granted disability pension or old-age pension under the State Pension Insurance Act, as well as to those who have been identified as having partial or no working capacity, and to insured persons over 63 years of age. The amount of dental benefit for these persons is 85 euros per year, i.e. the sickness fund pays up to 85% of their medical bills, calculated that the total amount of medical bills or invoices submitted to the patient does not initially exceed 100 euros per year. The patient's own contribution is 15%. In the case of larger invoices, the amount paid by the State will still remain at EUR 85 per year and the own contribution will simply increase. If you think that the sickness fund was EUR 19.18 in the past, the increase to EUR 85 should certainly encourage many to go to dentists. The dental prosthetic allowance, EUR 255.65 for three years, will remain. dental care benefits for pregnant women, mothers of children under one year of age and those with an increased need for treatment will also increase from EUR 28,77 per year to EUR 85, the same principle being that, in the case of a €100 treatment bill, the own contribution will be EUR 15, or 15%. For adults whose benefit is now zero euros, the sickness fund will pay 30 euros per year from the 60-euro medical bill, or 50% per year. It is not a large part of the cost, but it should still give the opportunity to go to the dentist, even to those who have not done so far. The most important thing, however, is that the state will at last also pay for dental care with general anesthesia if it is medically indicated to an adult. At first glance, this change may seem insignificant, but it is invaluable for all those who suffer from mental and behavioural disorders or nervous system diseases or who suffer from severe physical disability. Finally, it is possible to perform a traumatic procedure or extensive dental care at once. Moreover, now that people with deep mental disabilities are receiving dental care with general anaesthesia only in Tartu, negotiations with hospitals in Tallinn have now started so that they can start offering the same service in Northern Estonia in the new year. Of course, these changes do not yet bring a bright white smile to all Estonian people, but they are certainly a step in the right direction. It should not be forgotten that under the current scheme dental benefits should start to increase from year to year. I will certainly wake all parents of my children, but why not also family doctors who are on their list of young people under the age of 19 more actively send them to the dentist so that dental discomfort is minimal in adulthood. The Social Democrats support the adoption of bill 301 as a law and call on all good colleagues to do so. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U122	Today's sixth item on the agenda is the second reading of the draft Act 330 on the ratification of the Protocol to the Amendment of the Status of the Baltic Defence College and its staff to the Protocol between the Government of the Republic of Estonia, the Government of the Republic of Latvia and the Government of the Republic of Lithuania. ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U123	President-in-Office of the Council. Ladies and gentlemen, The bill has already been through this hall, but as once foreseen, it has to come back again. This draft was launched on 14 November this year and the first reading took place on 23 November. The deadline for submitting amendments was 5.05 p.m. on 7 December, but no amendments were tabled. The National Defence Committee discussed the draft before the second reading at its meeting on 8 December and unanimously approved it together with the text and explanatory memorandum. The bill changes the status of the Baltic Defence College and its staff. Pursuant to the 1999 Protocol, VAT concessions are granted to the College as an institution for the official use of goods purchased. However, the 1999 Protocol does not provide for the use of this VAT advantage by staff seconded to the College. The draft under discussion provides that the VAT paid on the durable goods listed in the Annex to the Protocol purchased by foreign staff seconded to the College for their personal use during the first year of service shall be reimbursed. The amendment does not concern the Estonian staff of the Baltic Defence College or the people hired from the labour market, but only those posted from abroad to the College. As the level of military education of the College depends on the staff seconded from abroad, the reimbursement of VAT contributes to improving the reputation of the College as an external service place. The payment of the allowance shall be made from the budget of the Ministry of Defence. This does not involve new costs for Estonia, but a slight reduction in revenue, which is marginal at national level, since the change affects about 22 people in the first year, and then an average of 7 people in the future. As of today, it is known that the Latvian Seim has ratified the draft under discussion. He's also been sending us a diplomatic note about it. It is a bit more complicated with Lithuania, as Lithuania is known to have held two rounds of parliamentary elections at the beginning and end of October this year, followed by a change of government. The new Lithuanian government took an oath yesterday, on 13 December in front of the Seim. Through diplomatic channels, we are aware that the process of approving this protocol has been initiated there. At its meeting on 8 December, the National Defence Committee decided by consensus that the draft would be placed on the plenary agenda of the Riigikogu for second reading by 14 December. On the basis of § 115 of the Riigikogu Law on Home and Working Procedures, the National Defence Committee decided by consensus that the Riigikogu would be proposed to the Riigikogu at second reading for final voting and adoption as a law. Thank you!

ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U126	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Honourable Members of the Riigikogu! According to the Act on the ratification and implementation of the Treaty establishing the European Stability Mechanism (ESM), it is again time for an annual review of the stability grants granted by the ESM and the participation of the Republic of Estonia in the ESM. As a preliminary point, I will mention some general facts about the activities of the ESM over the past period. The year 2016 has been peaceful and has brought good news. In order to complete Cyprus' adjustment programme, less money was needed than planned, Spain also made another voluntary repayment and has now repaid EUR 6.3 billion to the ESM. The Greek programme is proceeding as expected. It was confirmed in June that Greece had met the objectives set for the first survey of the programme. A second inspection is underway, which is expected to be completed in the near future. Despite the negative interest environment prevailing in the markets in 2015, ESM was able to make a profit of EUR 729,4 million. As in previous years, profits were allocated to the reserve fund. To date, EUR 1.43 billion has been raised in the reserve fund and this money is used to protect paid-up capital against possible losses. It is expected that the positive trend will continue until the end of 2016 and that the ESM will thus be able to further increase its reserves. I'm going to ESM now. Currently, Spain, Cyprus and Greece are ESM clients. Greece is the only one of them in the active phase. First of all, I'm talking about the programmes that have ended. The Spanish programme has already ended in 2013. The aim of the programme was to put the banking sector in order. Spain remains under post-programme surveillance, which means regular European Commission monitoring missions. The monitoring mission in October showed that the Spanish financial sector remains stable. The quality of the banks' assets has improved and the restructuring of the state-aided banks has almost been completed. Economic growth is recovering in Spain. Spain has paid EUR 6.3 million back to the ESM before the deadline from 2014 onwards, paid two repayments in 2015 and made one payment this year. Voluntary repayments made before the deadline are evidence that the ESM programme helped Spain to restructure the banking sector and supported the rapid recovery from the financial crisis. Cyprus's aid programme ended successfully in March this year. The programme aimed at restructuring the financial sector. As a result of improved economic performance and rapid recovery of the economic sector, Cyprus needed only 6.3 billion out of the EUR 9 billion initially planned. Just as Spain remains under the post-programme surveillance of Cyprus. The Commission's first monitoring mission took place in September. The reforms carried out during the programme have managed to improve the financial sector in Cyprus and the current growth rate in 2016 of 2.8% of GDP is above forecast. As a result of fiscal consolidation efforts, Cyprus is expected to meet its fiscal targets. The improvement in confidence in the banking sector is signalled by the ability of banks to increase deposits, improve liquidity and increase capital buffers. So it can be said that the reforms that have been made bear fruit. Now I'm talking about Greece. The Greek programme was approved in 2015 and will last for three years. The programme will amount to EUR 86 billion. Six interim payments have now been made to Greece, with a total of EUR 31.7 billion being paid out. Greece will use the loan to service public debt, restructure the banking sector and eliminate deficits. Greece will be helped to implement state governance reforms and improve the competitiveness of the economy. Greece met the targets set for the first survey in June of this year. It is worth noting from the programme's major achievements that Greece was able to recapitalise four systemic banks, with a total cost of EUR 5.4 billion for two banks, and two banks covered their additional capital needs with market resources. As a result, only EUR 4.6 billion of EUR 10 billion was used by Greece for the recapitalisation of systemic banks. Since 2009, the number of public employees in Greece has fallen by 25.9%. As a result, the payroll has decreased by 31.4%. In addition, Greece has carried out significant privatisations. For example, 14 regional airports have been privatized and Greece's largest port, the port of Pireus. In May, the Eurogroup agreed on general debt relief measures with two main objectives. Firstly, it was intended to make the loan repayment schedule more affordable for Greece, thereby ensuring that in the long term Greece would be able to serve the loan. Secondly, it was intended to respond to the IMF's comments on the alleged unsustainable nature of the Greek debt service and to persuade the IMF to join the Greek third aid programme. The IMF has not joined the programme, although the Commission and the European Central Bank are negotiating. Both the Commission and the European Central Bank, with the support of the Eurogroup, are trying to create a situation in which the IMF would have a desire and opportunity to join Greece's third aid programme in the future. In doing so, the Eurogroup excludes the possibility of writing down the principal of the debt and stresses that all debt relief measures must remain within the framework of European Union law and the ESM basic acts. In June, Greece fulfilled the precondition for debt relief measures, i.e. the first review was successfully completed. The following measures will therefore be taken gradually. First, the EFSF repayment profile will be adjusted to avoid large repayments in individual years, without changing the average weighted maturity of the loan. The EFSF and ESM funding strategy shall be implemented in such a way as to reduce the impact of possible interest rate fluctuations on Greece. A one-off increase in interest in 2017 for one instalment of the EFSF loan shall be waived. Following the completion of the ESM programme in 2018, the European Central Bank and the Commission will carry out an in-depth analysis of Greece's debt burden. Should it become apparent that Greece still needs debt relief, negotiations will start in the context of considering possible measures. I will now mention some important activities in 2017. Work will continue next year on Greece's third aid programme. This means that regular surveys and payments are carried out. The Riigikogu will be kept informed of the progress made in implementing the programmes through the European Union Affairs Committee. The results of the on-going second review are expected to be presented to the Riigikogu in January. At the annual meeting of the ESM, the Board of Governors approved a proposal for an in-depth evaluation of the ESM and EFSF programmes. The aim is to analyse the second programme of Spain, Ireland, Portugal, Cyprus and Greece. The third programme for Greece, which has not yet been completed, is currently not complete, so it is early to draw conclusions on the success of this programme. A working group of ESM staff and international experts will assess the effectiveness and design of the ESM and EFSF stability programmes and analyse the experience gained. The evaluation report shall be submitted at the next ESM annual meeting to the managers for approval and the results shall be published. At the same time, discussions on the role of the ESM in the changing euro area will continue next year. The aim is to make the most of the ESM's potential for two tasks. Firstly, the role of the Single Resolution Fund (SRF) protection mechanism, which is currently guaranteed by national credit lines, is envisaged for the ESM. Secondly, at the stage of deepening EMU cooperation, the development of the budgetary capacity or the stabilisation function of the euro area may be on the agenda. Finally, we are pleased to note that the ESM has effectively helped countries adjust their economies and supported important structural reforms in banking, labour market policy, pension systems and public administration at large. At the same time, the ESM is still able to maintain a high rating level and generate profits that are used as a security measure for paid-up capital. The profits earned are proof that the ESM is able to finance its activities independently without further burdening the Member States. Thus, the ESM has successfully fulfilled the role of ensuring financial stability in the euro area and will continue to be a guarantee that future crises will be effectively addressed. Honourable Riigikogu! The Government of the Republic has kept the Riigikogu informed of the activities of the ESM and we will continue to do so. This year, the Government of the Republic has given an overview of the state of play of Greece's third programme in the Riigikogu's European Union Affairs Committee. In July, we sent the ESM Annual Report to the Riigikogu for information, which gives a more accurate overview of the activities of the ESM in 2015. The next overview of Estonia's participation in the ESM will be presented in autumn of next year. Thank you for your attention and I hope that cooperation will continue in the process of dealing with ESM issues. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U155	Today's eighth item is the second reading of the State Legal Assistance Act initiated by the Government of the Republic and the Act amending other laws. I would like to ask Jaanus Karilaidi, Chairman of the Committee on Legal Affairs, to present the chair to the commission.
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U156	Ladies and gentlemen, I would like to begin by pointing out that the draft was initiated by the Government of the Republic on 28 September 2016. The first reading took place on 19 October and the deadline for submitting amendments, and by 2 November the Estonian Central Party Group tabled five amendments to the draft. No other requests or proposals for the draft were received. More specifically, the draft regulates the availability of first-level legal advice. The procedure for the legal aid competition will be changed, organised by the Ministry of Justice. In the future, the round of participants will expand, and the winner of the competition will be awarded for up to five years. There is no time limit for contracts under the current regime, but in practice contracts have been concluded for one or two years, which is an inefficiently short period. Under the current rules, the state's legal assistance service provider can only receive support if it is an NGO or foundation. According to the draft, it can be granted in the future to all legal entities, such as law firms. The Ministry of Justice will supervise the service. The draft provides for a better use of the resources allocated to the provision of state aid. For example, if a secondary offence can be resolved by a short procedure and the suspect does not apply for a lawyer, the state will no longer be forced to appoint a lawyer. Similarly, persons who have been assigned a representative in proceedings without a civil action without his own request shall be released from the obligation to reimburse the costs of legal aid. This amendment alleviates the situation of beneficiaries of State aid, as the amount of state aid must, in general, be reimbursed in whole or in part to the State at a later stage. In addition, the draft provides for a premium higher than usual for the Chairman of the Viru County Court to motivate judges in the standing service of other courts to stand as head of the Viru County Court and to transfer the national register of state and local government institutions to the Business Register Information System platform. The Committee on Legal Affairs prepared a second reading on 6 December 2016. Mr Urmas Reinsalu, Minister of Justice and Mr Gunnar Vaikmaa, Head of the Department of Justice Administration Policy, freelances, took part in the meeting as representatives of the initiator of the draft. Representatives of the Estonian Bar Association, the Notary Chamber and the Estonian Children's Protection Association were invited to the Legal Affairs Committee meeting during the drafting phase, who did not attend the meeting. During the discussion of the amendments, the Commission found support and substantial consideration in the amendments of the Estonian Centre Party Group, which will subsequently exclude the rejection of the application for state legal aid if the application is not submitted in Estonian or, by way of exception, in English, if the legal aid applicant is resident or established in another Member State of the European Union. In cooperation with the representatives of the initiator's committee, the amendments were formulated as amendments initiated by the Committee on Legal Affairs and the Estonian Central Party Group withdrew its amendments. At its meeting on 13 December, attended by Minister of Justice Urmas Reinsalu, Deputy Secretary General Marko Aavik of the Ministry of Justice and Head of the Office for Freedoms at the Department of Justice Administration Policy, Gunnar Vaikmaa, the Committee on Legal Affairs amended the wording of its proposal initiated on 6 December 2016 and decided to initiate an additional amendment necessary for the operation of state legal aid in the event of a failure to call for a national legal aid provider. In total, three amendments have been tabled to the draft, all of which are initiated by the Committee on Legal Affairs and each of which is further explained in the explanatory memorandum to the second reading of the draft. I'm not going to read them now for the sake of saving time. The Committee on Legal Affairs decided to send the draft for second reading on the plenary agenda of the Riigikogu by 14 December 2016 and to propose that the second reading be terminated. When the second reading is completed, we will have a proposal to send the draft to the third reading by 15 December 2016 and to put it to the final vote. Thank you!

ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U226	Good vice chairman! Indeed, this bill changes a number of things. Applications for legal aid, where there is a factual, personal issue, or if it has not been possible to understand whether a person understands why he or she is applying for legal aid, have been rejected. The Government of the Republic is, as we know, an institution that does not change with the change of coalition partner. The position clearly states that the Government of the Republic does not support this amendment, firstly because the official language of the Republic of Estonia and, consequently, the language of legal proceedings are Estonian, and secondly because in practice there is no problem of access to state legal aid due to insufficient language skills. In practice, no application for legal aid has been rejected as a result of non-compliance with the language requirement. The same government has always explained that in practice we do not have such a problem. Andres Anvelt, for example, who, as I recall, was the leader of the political group at the time, made such an assessment that the lack of access to justice through the possibility of state legal aid does not depend on nationality or language, but is a much wider problem. In his opinion, the problem begins with the fact that the application and the accompanying documents do not prove the person's need for free legal aid. ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U260	Ladies and gentlemen, § 9 of the Constitution of the Republic of Estonia states that the rights, freedoms and obligations of all and everyone listed in the Constitution are equal for Estonian citizens and foreign citizens and stateless persons present in Estonia. According to paragraph 12, everyone is equal before the law, no one must be discriminated against because of nationality, race, colour, sex, language, origin, religion, political or other beliefs, property and social status, or other circumstances. Paragraph 13 states that everyone has the right to the protection of the state and the law. Paragraph 14 states that ensuring rights and freedoms is the responsibility of the legislative, executive and judicial authorities and local authorities. According to paragraph 15, everyone has the right to apply to the courts in the event of violation of their rights and freedoms. Everyone has Estonian citizens, foreign citizens and stateless persons present in Estonia. The famous Italian constitutiveist Piero Calamandrei has said that the Constitution is not a perpetuation of a mob that moves forward once launched, but the Constitution is like a piece of paper: if you let it fall, it will fall down and it will not move. In order for the Constitution to move, fuel must be added every day, commitment must be added, soul must be added, promises must be kept, and there must be a desire to keep promises and feel their own responsibility. Our constitution is, in the same way, a reality in only one part; in the other part, it is a programme that has been written down and which we must comply with. It is an ideal, a hope and a duty to keep working. What we're doing today is work. This bill gives fuel to our beautiful constitution. We say that a person has the right to legal aid, regardless of his or her mother tongue. Whether he or she is an Estonian citizen, a foreign national or a stateless person, he or she is entitled to the protection of the law, because we at least want to be a state of law. This bill does not change the language of the hearing, but gives the right to apply for legal aid in other languages as well. We're showing you that we're not arrogant. We show that we remember how bad it was to live in a country where your language was always under attack and you never knew whether you could speak your language or not, where the law was not always available to you in your own language. Now we have our own country, we have a free country, and we can do better. Are we gonna do this? Today, with this bill, we show that we are better. The draft simply gives the opportunity to fill out one form in a language other than Estonian. This can be done in Russian, but it can also be done in other languages, for example in Ukrainian. If the court or anyone else who is going to process this form thinks that there are a sufficient number of residents in Estonia who speak Ukrainian, the form filled in in in Ukrainian will also be accepted and translated, but all other legal proceedings will proceed in Estonian according to the law. It must also be borne in mind that this amendment applies only to civil and administrative courts. After all, it is precisely the rules that we now come to in administrative and civil proceedings: people have a translation, there are judicial interpreters, and so on. The other documentation will continue to be in Estonian and will remain in Estonian. This unfortunate application form, which has given rise to so much controversy and foam here today, will become Estonian, with the Russian original document accompanied by an Estonian translation, so that the judges, who do not speak this other language, but only Estonian, will have no difficulty in processing this document and organising a further trial. I do think that the Estonian state will not become a much worse place to live because of this bill. It will continue to be as good a country as it has been so far. ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U302	Today's ninth item is the second reading of the draft law amending the Penal Code initiated by the Government of the Republic and related amendments to other laws (transposition of the freezing and confiscation of instrumentalities and proceeds of crime). On behalf of the commission, I would like to ask for a presentation of the Chair's member of the Committee on Legal Affairs, Raivo Aegis.
ParlaMint-EE_2016-12-14_U303	Ladies and gentlemen, I would remind you of what this is about: the draft will transpose the directive on the freezing and confiscation of instrumentalities and proceeds of crime and, moreover, the procedures relating to evidence, seized property and findings will be organised. At its meeting, the Committee on Legal Affairs discussed 308 draft laws submitted by the Government of the Republic for the first time on 8 November, including at first reading on 23 November. The deadline for submitting amendments to the draft law was set at 4 p.m. on 2 December. By the deadline laid down, amendments were received by the Legal Affairs Committee from the Ministry of Justice and the Chamber of Law Enforcement and Bankruptcy Administrators. The Committee on Legal Affairs dealt with the amendments received at its meeting on 5 December. I will now give more details on the amendments. A total of five points were received from the Ministry of Justice. Amendment 1 relates to the amendment of the Penal Code. Paragraph 1 of the draft law amending the Law on penalties is supplemented by point 17 and the numbering of the following points is also changed. The new point 17 refers to the text of the draft text as follows: '§ 184 (5) (2), 185 (4) (2), 255 (2) (2), 256 (2) (2), 268 (2) (2), 414 (5) (2) and 415 (3) (2) of the Code are replaced by 'the court may apply'; What's the point? This is precisely because the amendment removes the mismatch between the provisions of §832 (1) of the Penal Code and the provisions of the Special Part of the Penal Code. Pursuant to § 832 (1) of the Penal Code, the obligation to confiscate has been extended to a discretionary decision ('... the court... '). On the other hand, the provisions of the special section laying down the possibility of extended confiscation are worded in a compulsory form, i.e. imperatively ('the Court applies...'). This amendment will eliminate this contradiction and create a situation where discretion is left to the court to decide on the matter. Amendment 2 concerns the Code of Misdemeanour Procedure and reads as follows: The current text of § 3 of the draft law is considered to be paragraph 1 and the paragraph is supplemented by paragraphs 2 to 5. Paragraph 2 reads as follows: 'paragraph 2071 (1) and subsection 2104 (1) replace the word 'prefecture' with 'criminal care department'. ' Item 3: 'paragraph 2071 (2) and subsections 2104 (2) and (3) 'prefect' is replaced by 'Head of the Criminal Care Department'. ' Item 4: 'in paragraph 2101, § 2102 and § 2103 the words 'Police and Border Guard Board' are replaced by 'criminal care officer'. ' Point 5: 'Chapter 18 of the Code is supplemented by § 2123 as follows: '§ 2123. Transfer of community service. A court judgment or order enforceable until 1 January 2017 on the punishment of the accused with community service which has not been completed or partially completed shall be transmitted to the probation department within 30 days from 1 January 2017 until 30 January 2017. '' What is the purpose or content of this amendment? Under the current rules, the public service of the guilty is organised by two agencies: the Police and Border Guard Board and the probation department attached to the prisons. The difference is that the Police and Border Guard Board applies community service to those who have been convicted of misdemeanours, while the probation department applies it to those who have been convicted of criminal offences. The draft stipulates that the organisation of community service for misdemeanours will also be transferred to the Ministry of Justice's administration, probation departments. Amendment 3: 'Replace the word 'January' in § 4 (2) of the draft with the word 'February'. "In accordance with § 4 (2) of the draft law, § 102 (7) of the State Assets Act is added, which states that assets confiscated before 1 January 2017 are subject to the provisions of the law in force at the time of confiscation. This provision is necessary in order to allow for further processing of property already seized by rural governments at the time of the entry into force of the legislative amendment and for which the procedure has not been completed. According to the draft law, the assets to be dealt with by the county governments will not be transferred to the Police and Border Guard Board or the Tax and Customs Board, but will be executed by the institution to which they have been transferred according to the law in force. Amendment 4 complements the draft with § 7 'amendment of the Criminal Care Act', which supplements § 2 (6) of the Penal Care Act with the following wording: 'guilty whose arrest has been replaced by community service. ' Since the bill, as we have heard before, proposes that the organisation of community service applied for misdemeanour be taken over by the probation departments, the perpetrators who have been punished in misdemeanour proceedings will also be under the supervision of the probation officers. As § 2 of the Penal Care Act lists everyone who is in contact with the probation officers, those responsible who have been replaced by community service, or who have been punished by a misdemeanour procedure, must also be added to the list. Amendment 5: 'Reform and formulate § 8 (former § 7) as follows: § 8. Entry into force of the law. (1) Paragraphs 2 (3) to (5) and 15 of this Act, § 3 (1) and 4 to (6) of this Act shall enter into force on 1 February 2017. (2) Paragraph 3 (2) (5) and Article 7 of this Act shall enter into force on the day following their publication in the Official Journal of the State. ' The content of the amendment is as follows: The first point is necessary in order to ensure that the transfer of seized property, findings and evidence can be carried out smoothly and without problems between the Police and Border Guard Board, the Tax and Customs Board and the county authorities. Since the adoption of the law has shifted so far to the end of the year, the entry into force of the law on 1 January would be problematic and it is therefore proposed to extend this deadline for a month. Point 2 of the amendment is necessary for the Police and Border Guard Board to be able to hand over the organisation of community service to the Ministry of Justice as soon as possible, as these resources are already foreseen by the state budget for the Ministry of Justice in the 2017 budget. It would therefore be useful and necessary to transfer to them the right under the law to organise community service for those who have been convicted of misdemeanour. The Steering Committee has proposed that all of these five amendments be fully taken into account; these proposals have been supported by consensus. As I pointed out earlier, in addition to the Ministry of Justice's proposal, the Committee on Legal Affairs received a proposal from the Chamber of Law Enforcement and Bankruptcy Administrators. This was that instead of the Police and Border Guard Board and the Tax and Customs Board, who took over the affairs of the county authorities regarding the realisation of evidence, confiscated property and findings, they could be dealt with by bailiffs. As we know, we have only recently dealt with bill No 280, the subject of which was that the so-called expected profits of bailiffs could be significantly reduced, because the prescribed state fees have decreased and the fullers will no longer receive as much revenue as before. The Commission had a brief discussion on this subject, but it was decided that it would be reasonable to deal with this possible referral to bailiffs together with this bill 280. In the Committee on Legal Affairs, the proposal of the Law Enforcement Tribunals and the Chamber of Bankruptcy Administrators was not supported. The Commission decided to propose that the draft be placed on the plenary agenda by 14 December 2016 and that the second reading be closed and that the draft be sent to third reading when the second reading is completed, by 15 December 2016 and put to the final vote. The fifth point in the decision was that Raivo Time was appointed rapporteur for the Commission. All these decisions were taken by consensus. Thank you.
